\title[Anomalous scaling limit]{Anomalous scaling limit of a Brownian particle in a log-correlated potential}

\author[S. Armstrong]{Scott Armstrong}

\date{}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
We study a Brownian particle in~$\Rd$,~$d\geq2$, with drift given by the gradient of a log-correlated Gaussian potential. At weak disorder, we prove convergence to a scaling limit whose law is singular with respect to Brownian motion and whose invariant measure is given by Gaussian multiplicative chaos. The proof uses a renormalization group induction: at each scale, the elliptic operator is well approximated by a Laplacian with effective diffusivity decaying as a power of scale. We compute this exponent exactly in dimension two.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

\section{Introduction}

\subsection{The diffusion and its scaling limit}

We study the long-time behavior of a Brownian particle in~$\Rd$,~$d\geq2$, whose drift is the gradient of a log-correlated Gaussian potential~$\log\a$. The particle position~$X_t$ is described by the stochastic differential equation
\begin{equation}
	dX_t
	=
	\nabla\log\a(X_t)\,dt+\sqrt2\,dW_t\,,
	\qquad
	X_0=x\in\Rd\,,
	\label{e.sde.intro}
\end{equation}
where~$W$ is a standard Brownian motion on~$\Rd$.

\smallskip

For a bounded stationary potential with sufficiently rapid decay of correlations, homogenization theory gives a Brownian scaling limit with a deterministic positive effective diffusivity. In the model considered here, the potential has independent fluctuations of equal strength at every scale. As more scales are included, their effects accumulate and the effective diffusivity tends to zero.

\smallskip

In this paper, we prove that, at weak disorder, the effective diffusivity decays at least as a power of the length scale. The particle is therefore \textbf{subdiffusive}: the time needed to travel a large distance grows faster than the square of that distance. We also prove convergence to a unique scaling limit and show that its path law is singular with respect to Brownian motion.

\smallskip

We construct the potential from independent fields~$\g_0,\g_1,\g_2,\ldots$ at scales~$1,3,3^2,\ldots$. The field~$\g_0$ is smooth, stationary, centered and Gaussian, with finite range of dependence. The field~$\g_k$ has the law of~$\g_0(3^{-k}\,\cdot)$: it is a copy of~$\g_0$ stretched by the factor~$3^k$. We denote the size of these fluctuations by the \textbf{disorder strength}~$\gmcdelta\in(0,\nf12]$ and set
\begin{equation}
	\a(x)
	\coloneqq
	\exp\left(
	\sum_{k=0}^\infty(\g_k(x)-\g_k(0))
	\right)\,.
	\label{e.intro.anchored.coefficient}
\end{equation}
Subtracting the values at the origin makes the series converge locally in~$C^{1,1}$.

\smallskip

The logarithmic correlations come from counting scales. Fields with scale much smaller than~$|x-y|$ take independent values at~$x$ and~$y$, whereas fields with much larger scale change little between the two points. The number of contributing scales grows logarithmically with the distance. Thus, for~$|x-y|\geq2$, the variance of~$\log\a(x)-\log\a(y)$ lies between two positive multiples of~$\log|x-y|$, depending only on~$d$ and the law of~$\g_0$.

\smallskip

There are two sources of randomness. We write~$\P$ and~$\E$ for the law and expectation of the environment~$\omega$, a realization of the fields. The law of the trajectories in a fixed environment is called \textbf{quenched}; its average over the environment is called \textbf{annealed}.

\smallskip

Our main result holds when~$\gmcdelta$ is smaller than a threshold depending only on~$d$. To state it informally, let~$\ahom_N$ be the effective diffusivity obtained by retaining only~$\g_0,\ldots,\g_N$ and normalizing the coefficient to have mean one; the precise definition is given after~\eqref{e.intro.cutoff.coefficients}. Let~$\tau^2\coloneqq\log\E[\exp(\g_0(0))]$. For the particle started at the origin, Theorem~\ref{t.A} and our estimate of~$\log\ahom_N$ give, roughly,
\begin{equation}
	\bigl(3^{-N}X_{3^{2N}t/\ahom_N}\bigr)_{t\geq0}
	\longrightarrow
	(Z_t)_{t\geq0}
	\qquad\text{with}\qquad
	\ahom_N\approx e^{-\nf{2\tau^2(N+1)}{d}}
	\quad\text{as } N\to\infty\,,
	\label{e.intro.roughly}
\end{equation}
where the approximation describes the logarithm of~$\ahom_N$; its error is given in~\eqref{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic}. The limit~$Z$ is a continuous, conservative, strong Markov diffusion.

\smallskip

The assumptions below also allow non-Gaussian fields. Fix~$d\geq2$ and~$\gmcdelta\in(0,\nf12]$, and let~$\g_0,\g_1,\g_2,\ldots$ be independent random fields such that, for every~$k\in\N_0$,
\begin{equation}
	\g_k
	\quad\text{and}\quad
	\g_0(3^{-k}\,\cdot)
	\quad\text{have the same law}\,.
	\label{e.same.law.shift}
\end{equation}
For~$m\in\Z$, let~$\cu_m\coloneqq(-\frac12\,3^m,\frac12\,3^m)^d$ be the cube of side~$3^m$; in particular,~$\cu_0=(-\nf12,\nf12)^d$. We use the maximum norm for spatial cubes, balls and their geometry. In~\ref{a.g2}, the gradient has the dual norm, and the Hessian norm is the Lipschitz constant of the derivative on~$\cu_0$ with respect to these norms.

\smallskip

Quadratic energies and variational formulas use the Euclidean pairing~$p\cdot q=\sum_{i=1}^d p_iq_i$ and magnitude~$|p|_2=(p\cdot p)^{\nf12}$, including for gradients and fluxes; unit vectors in these formulas are Euclidean unit vectors. Matrix magnitudes in the coarse-graining formulas are Euclidean operator norms. The range-of-dependence distance in~\ref{a.g1} and the fractional Sobolev and H\"older conventions below are also Euclidean. We assume the following.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\textbf{g\arabic*})]
\setcounter{enumi}{0}

\item
\label{a.g1}
The field~$\g_0$ is stationary, has mean zero, and has range of dependence~$\sqrt d$, measured in Euclidean distance: the sigma-fields generated by its restrictions to measurable sets~$U,V\subseteq\Rd$ are independent whenever~$|x-y|_2\geq\sqrt d$ for every~$x\in U$ and~$y\in V$.

\item
\label{a.g2}
Almost surely,~$\g_0\in C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Rd)$, and
\begin{equation}
	\E\left[\exp\left(\gmcdelta^{-2}\left(\|\g_0\|_{L^\infty(\cu_0)}+\|\nabla\g_0\|_{L^\infty(\cu_0)}+\|\nabla^2\g_0\|_{L^\infty(\cu_0)}\right)^2\right)\right]\leq2\,.
	\label{e.coefficient.field.regularity}
\end{equation}
\item
\label{a.g3}
The law of~$\g_0$ is invariant under signed permutations of the coordinates and under~$\g_0\mapsto-\g_0$.

\item
\label{a.g4}
The number~$\tau^2=\log\E[\exp(\g_0(0))]$ is positive.
\end{enumerate}
Assumption~\ref{a.g2} says that the suprema of~$|\g_0|$,~$|\nabla\g_0|$ and~$|\nabla^2\g_0|$ on a unit cube are of order~$\gmcdelta$, with Gaussian tails. Together with the symmetry~$\g_0\mapsto-\g_0$ in~\ref{a.g3}, it gives~$C(d)<\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}
	\tau^2\leq C\gmcdelta^2\,,
	\label{e.tau.bound}
\end{equation}
and~$\bigl|\tau^2-\frac12\E[|\g_0(0)|^2]\bigr|\leq C\gmcdelta^4$. We say that a result holds \textbf{at weak disorder} if it holds whenever~$\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, for some~$\gmcdelta_0>0$ depending only on~$d$ and the indicated parameters.

\smallskip

Throughout,~$X$ denotes the continuous Feller diffusion with generator~$\a^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\a\nabla)$. We construct it from its resolvent in Section~\ref{s.fixed.coefficient} and show that, from every starting point, its law is that of the solution of~\eqref{e.sde.intro}. Its reversible measure is~$\a(x)\,dx$.

\smallskip

For~$L\in\N_0$, we introduce an \textbf{infrared cutoff} by retaining the fields with scales at most~$3^L$. The number~$3^L$ is the \textbf{cutoff scale}, and the corresponding coefficient is
\begin{equation}
	\a_L(x)
	\coloneqq
	\exp\left(
	\sum_{k=0}^L(\g_k(x)-\tau^2)
	\right)\,.
	\label{e.intro.cutoff.coefficients}
\end{equation}
This coefficient is stationary, has mean one and has range of dependence of order~$3^L$. Subtracting~$\tau^2$ normalizes the mean. As in~\eqref{e.intro.anchored.coefficient}, subtracting constants from the fields leaves the drift unchanged. Let~$X^{(L)}$ be the diffusion constructed with~$\a_L$ in place of~$\a$; its generator is
\begin{equation}
	\a_L^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\a_L\nabla)
	=
	\Delta+\nabla\log\a_L\cdot\nabla\,.
	\label{e.intro.cutoff.generator}
\end{equation}
The homogenized matrix of~$\a_L$ describes the equation on domains much larger than~$3^L$: replacing~$\a_L$ by this constant matrix gives nearly the same solutions. By~\ref{a.g3}, the matrix is a scalar multiple of the identity. We denote the scalar by~$\ahom_L$ and call it the \textbf{effective diffusivity}.

\smallskip

At weak disorder, this diffusivity also describes motion on the cutoff scale itself. The annealed mean time for~$X^{(L)}$ to leave a cube of side~$3^L$, when started at its center, is of order~$3^{2L}/\ahom_L$. The same is true of~$X$ without cutoff (Lemma~\ref{lim:lem-mean-exit}). This explains the time scale~$3^{2N}/\ahom_N$ in~\eqref{e.intro.roughly}.

\smallskip

Theorem~\ref{t.renormalized.diffusivities} shows that~$\ahom_m$ is nonincreasing, is bounded below by~$e^{-(m+1)\tau^2}$, and is bounded above by a negative power of~$3^{m+1}$ whose exponent depends only on~$d$ and the law of~$\g_0$. At weak disorder we identify the leading decay rate: there exist~$\gmcdelta_0(d)>0$ and~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, if~$\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, then, for every~$m\in\N_0$,
\begin{equation}
	\left|
	\log\ahom_m+\frac{2\tau^2(m+1)}d
	\right|
	\leq
	C\gmcdelta^2|\log\gmcdelta|(1+m\gmcdelta)\,.
	\label{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic}
\end{equation}
In dimension two, planar duality gives the exact formula~$\ahom_m=e^{-(m+1)\tau^2}$ for every~$m\in\N_0$. Rotation by a right angle exchanges gradients and divergence-free fields, so the homogenized matrices of~$\a_m$ and~$\a_m^{-1}$ are inverse to each other. The symmetry~$\g_0\mapsto-\g_0$ gives~$\a_m^{-1}$ the law of~$e^{2(m+1)\tau^2}\a_m$, which determines~$\ahom_m$. The time scale in~\eqref{e.intro.roughly} is then exactly~$3^{2N}e^{(N+1)\tau^2}$.

\smallskip

When~$m\gmcdelta\geq1$ and~$\tau^2$ is at least a fixed positive multiple of~$\gmcdelta^2$, the error in~\eqref{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic} is smaller than the leading term by a factor of order~$\gmcdelta|\log\gmcdelta|$.

\smallskip

\begin{theoremA}[Scaling limit]
\label{t.A}
Assume~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}. There exists~$\gmcdelta_0(d)>0$ such that, if~$0<\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, then there is a continuous strong Markov process~$Z$ on~$\Rd$ in a random environment, with quenched laws~$\mathbf P_x$ and expectations~$\mathbf E_x$ when started at~$x$, such that the following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \underline{\emph{Convergence}}: For every~$x\in\Rd$, as the integer~$N$ tends to infinity, the annealed law of the process~$(3^{-N}X_{3^{2N}t/\ahom_N})_{t\geq0}$ with~$X_0=3^Nx$ converges to the annealed law of~$Z$ started at~$x$.
\item \underline{\emph{Reversible measure}}: Almost surely,~$Z$ is reversible with respect to a measure~$\mu$ that is singular with respect to Lebesgue measure.
\item \underline{\emph{Anomalous scaling}}: There exist~$C<\infty$ and~$\eta>0$, depending only on~$d$ and the law of~$\g_0$, such that, if~$\sigma_r$ denotes the first time at which~$Z$ leaves the cube of side~$r$ centered at its starting point, then, for every~$x\in\Rd$ and~$r\in(0,1]$,
\begin{equation*}
	\E\bigl[\mathbf E_x[\sigma_r]\bigr]\leq Cr^{2+\eta}\,.
\end{equation*}
For every~$x\in\Rd$, almost surely,~$\mathbf P_x$-almost every path satisfies
\begin{equation*}
	\limsup_{t\downarrow0}\,t^{-\gamma}|Z_t-Z_0|=\infty
	\qquad\text{for every }\gamma>\frac1{2+\eta}\,.
\end{equation*}
In dimension two, one can take~$\eta=\tau^2/\log3$.
\item \underline{\emph{Non-Gaussian marginals}}: Almost surely, for every~$x\in\Rd$ and~$t>0$, the law of~$Z_t$ under~$\mathbf P_x$ is absolutely continuous with respect to~$\mu$ and is not Gaussian.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theoremA}

Theorem~\ref{t.scaling.limit} gives the stronger, quenched version of~(i). We realize the rescaled processes in one environment, and their quenched laws converge almost surely. Rescaling space by~$3^{-N}$ moves the field at scale~$3^k$ to scale~$3^{k-N}$. The common environment therefore contains independent fields at every scale~$3^j$, with~$j\in\Z$.

\smallskip

The reversible measures of these rescaled processes, suitably normalized, converge almost surely to~$\mu$. For Gaussian fields, this is the usual construction of Gaussian multiplicative chaos: exponentiate the smoothed field, divide by its mean, and let the smoothing scale tend to zero. More precisely,~$\mu$ is a stationary chaos measure multiplied by a positive continuous factor from the fields at scales larger than one.

\smallskip

The slow motion of~$X$ over large distances becomes rapid motion of~$Z$ over small distances. Brownian motion needs time of order~$r^2$ to leave a cube of side~$r$, whereas~(iii) bounds the annealed mean exit time of~$Z$ by~$Cr^{2+\eta}$. These rapid exits force its pointwise H\"older exponent at time zero to be at most~$1/(2+\eta)$, below the Brownian value~$\nf12$.

\smallskip

By the law of the iterated logarithm, the quenched path law~$\mathbf P_x$ is therefore singular with respect to every mixture of laws of Brownian motions. The same roughness bound also gives singularity of the annealed path law. For Gaussian~$\g_0$, we have~$\tau^2=\frac12\operatorname{Var}[\g_0(0)]$; in dimension two the bound on the H\"older exponent is thus~$\bigl(2+\operatorname{Var}[\g_0(0)]/(2\log3)\bigr)^{-1}$.

\subsection{Quantitative homogenization}

We study the particle through its elliptic operator. After dividing the cutoff coefficient by its effective diffusivity, we compare the corresponding Dirichlet problem with the one for the Laplacian. At weak disorder, the error is small uniformly in the cutoff, even on a domain of the same size as the cutoff scale. For~$0\leq L\leq M$, define~$A_{L,M}(x)\coloneqq\ahom_L^{-1}\a_L(3^Mx)$ for~$x\in\cu_0$. The coefficient~$A_{L,M}$ is stationary, has homogenized matrix equal to the identity, and has range of dependence of order~$3^{L-M}$. Given~$f\in L^2(\cu_0)$ and~$h\in H^2(\cu_0)$, let~$u_{L,M},u_{\mathrm{hom}}\in H^1(\cu_0)$ solve
\begin{equation*}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		-\nabla\cdot(A_{L,M}\nabla u_{L,M})&=f
		&&\text{in }\cu_0\,,\\
		u_{L,M}&=h
		&&\text{on }\partial \cu_0\,,
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
	\qquad
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		-\Delta u_{\mathrm{hom}}&=f
		&&\text{in }\cu_0\,,\\
		u_{\mathrm{hom}}&=h
		&&\text{on }\partial \cu_0\,,
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
\end{equation*}
and measure the errors in the solution, its gradient and its flux by the quantity below. Here~$H^{-1}$ denotes the dual of~$H^1_0$, with the volume-normalized, componentwise zero-boundary norm of~\eqref{e.ordinary.negative.norm.convention}; since~$|\cu_0|=1$, we omit the underline:
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal D_{L,M}
	\coloneqq
	\|u_{L,M}-u_{\mathrm{hom}}\|_{L^2(\cu_0)}
	+
	\|\nabla u_{L,M}-\nabla u_{\mathrm{hom}}\|_{H^{-1}(\cu_0)}
	+
	\|A_{L,M}\nabla u_{L,M}-\nabla u_{\mathrm{hom}}\|_{H^{-1}(\cu_0)}\,.
\end{equation*}
We suppress the dependence of~$u_{L,M}$ and~$\mathcal D_{L,M}$ on~$f$ and~$h$. The first estimate below is uniform in the cutoff: for fixed~$\vartheta\in(0,1)$ and~$q\in[1,\infty)$, at weak disorder,~$\mathcal D_{L,M}$ is at most~$\gmcdelta^\vartheta$ times the norms of the data and a random factor whose~$L^q(\P)$ norm is bounded uniformly in~$L$ and~$M$. The second gives, up to a random factor, the algebraic rate~$3^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{hom}}(M-L)}$, for some~$\alpha_{\mathrm{hom}}(d)>0$, when the cutoff is fixed and the domain grows.

\begin{theoremA}[Quantitative homogenization of the Dirichlet problem]
\label{t.B}
Assume~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}. The negative norms in~$\mathcal D_{L,M}$ are the duals of~$H^1_0$, with the componentwise zero-boundary convention of~\eqref{e.ordinary.negative.norm.convention}. The following statements hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \underline{\emph{Estimate uniform in the cutoff}}: For every~$\vartheta\in(0,1)$ and~$q\in[1,\infty)$, there exist~$\gmcdelta_0(\vartheta,q,d)>0$ and~$C(\vartheta,q,d)<\infty$ such that, if~$\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, then there exist random variables~$\mathcal Z_{L,M}\geq1$ satisfying
\begin{equation*}
	\sup_{0\leq L\leq M}
	\|\mathcal Z_{L,M}\|_{L^q(\P)}
	\leq C
\end{equation*}
such that, almost surely, for every~$L$,~$M$,~$f$ and~$h$,
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal D_{L,M}
	\leq
	\mathcal Z_{L,M}\,\gmcdelta^\vartheta
	\left(\|f\|_{L^2(\cu_0)}+\|h\|_{H^2(\cu_0)}\right)\,.
\end{equation*}
If~$f=0$, then the factor~$\gmcdelta^\vartheta$ may be replaced by~$C\gmcdelta$.

\item \underline{\emph{Algebraic rate at a fixed cutoff}}: There exists~$\alpha_{\mathrm{hom}}(d)>0$ such that, for every~$L\in\N_0$ and~$q\in[1,\infty)$, there exists a random variable~$\mathcal Y_{L,q}\geq1$ with~$\|\mathcal Y_{L,q}\|_{L^q(\P)}\leq C(L,q,d,\gmcdelta)$ such that, almost surely, for every~$M\geq L$ and every~$f$ and~$h$,
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal D_{L,M}
	\leq
	\mathcal Y_{L,q}\,3^{-\alpha_{\mathrm{hom}}(M-L)}
	\left(\|f\|_{L^2(\cu_0)}+\|h\|_{H^2(\cu_0)}\right)\,.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theoremA}

The estimate in~(i) includes~$M=L$, when the range of dependence is comparable to the size of the domain. Its small parameter is the disorder strength, and its constants are uniform in the cutoff. In~(ii), the cutoff is fixed and enlarging the domain gives an algebraic rate; weak disorder is not required. These estimates are proved in Theorems~\ref{t.cutoff.Dirichlet.homogenization} and~\ref{t.fixed.cutoff.Dirichlet.algebraic} of Section~\ref{s.Holder.regularity}.

\smallskip

There is a probabilistic interpretation. For smooth data,~$u_{L,M}$ describes the diffusion with generator~$\nabla\cdot(A_{L,M}\nabla)$, a time change of~$X^{(L)}$ with space rescaled by~$3^{-M}$. Taking~$f=1$ and~$h=0$ gives its expected exit time from~$\cu_0$; taking~$f=0$ gives the expected value of~$h$ at the exit position. Theorem~\ref{t.B} compares these quantities with their Brownian counterparts in~$L^2(\cu_0)$.

\smallskip

For the original particle, the generator also contains the factor~$\a^{-1}$. Its law is determined by the energy~$\int\a|\nabla u|^2\,dx$ together with the reversible measure~$\a\,dx$. Constructing the scaling limit requires convergence of both. The bound in Theorem~\ref{t.B}(i) remains small but does not tend to zero with the cutoff, so it does not establish convergence of the energies. The proof of Theorem~\ref{t.A} proceeds through the regularity and uniqueness arguments described below; it does not use Theorem~\ref{t.B}.

\subsection{Large-scale regularity and a Liouville theorem}

The next result controls the oscillation of~$\a_L$-harmonic functions, that is, solutions of~$\nabla\cdot(\a_L\nabla u)=0$. We obtain H\"older estimates with exponents up to~$1-C_0\gmcdelta|\log\gmcdelta|^{\nf12}$, where~$C_0$ depends only on~$d$. The estimates are uniform in the cutoff, and the exponents approach one as~$\gmcdelta\to0$. The largest of these exponents also gives a Liouville theorem: almost surely, for every cutoff~$L$, an~$\a_L$-harmonic function on~$\Rd$ that grows more slowly than~$|x|^{1-C_0\gmcdelta|\log\gmcdelta|^{\nf12}}$ is constant.

\smallskip

For each exponent, a random number of scales must separate the cube where the function is harmonic from the subcubes where it is estimated. This number has an exponential tail, uniformly in~$L$. We use the notation~$(u)_U\coloneqq\fint_Uu$ and~$\|u\|_{\underline L^p(U)}\coloneqq(\fint_U|u|^p)^{\nf1p}$ for a measurable set~$U$ of finite positive volume and~$p\in[1,\infty)$.

\begin{theoremA}[Large-scale H\"older regularity and a Liouville theorem]
\label{t.C}
Assume~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}. There exist~$\gmcdelta_0(d)>0$,~$C_0(d)<\infty$ and~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, if~$\gmcdelta\leq\gmcdelta_0$, then the following statements hold with
\begin{equation*}
	\gamma_{\mathrm{reg}}
	\coloneqq
	1-C_0\gmcdelta|\log\gmcdelta|^{\nf12}\,.
\end{equation*}
In both statements,~$L$ ranges over~$\N_0\cup\{\infty\}$, and~$\a_L$ is read as~$\a$ when~$L=\infty$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \underline{\emph{Large-scale H\"older estimate}}: Let~$\gamma\in[\nf12,\gamma_{\mathrm{reg}}]$. For every~$L$ and~$m\in\N$, there exists an~$\N$-valued random variable~$\mathcal L_L(\gamma,m)$ such that, for every~$k\in\N$,
\begin{equation}
	\P[\mathcal L_L(\gamma,m)>k]
	\leq
	C\exp\left(
	-\frac{(1-\gamma)^2(k-C)_+}
	{C\gmcdelta^2|\log\gmcdelta|}
	\right)\,.
	\label{e.intro.regularity.minimal.scale}
\end{equation}
Moreover, almost surely, for every~$L$ and~$m$, if~$u\in H^1(\cu_m)$ is~$\a_L$-harmonic in~$\cu_m$, then, for every~$n\in\N_0$ satisfying~$n\leq m-\mathcal L_L(\gamma,m)$ and every~$z\in3^n\Zd$ with~$z+\cu_n\subseteq\cu_{m-1}$,
\begin{equation}
	\left\|
	u-(u)_{z+\cu_n}
	\right\|_{\underline L^2(z+\cu_n)}
	\leq
	C3^{-\gamma(m-n)}
	\left\|
	u-(u)_{\cu_m}
	\right\|_{\underline L^2(\cu_m)}
	\label{e.intro.large.scale.Holder}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
	\left\|\a_L^{\nf12}\nabla u
	\right\|_{\underline L^2(z+\cu_n)}
	\leq
	C3^{(1-\gamma)(m-n)}
	\left\|\a_L^{\nf12}\nabla u
	\right\|_{\underline L^2(\cu_m)}\,.
	\label{e.intro.large.scale.energy}
\end{equation}

\item \underline{\emph{Liouville theorem}}: Almost surely, for every~$L$, if~$u\in H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Rd)$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}
	-\nabla\cdot(\a_L\nabla u)=0
	\quad\text{in }\Rd
	\quad\text{and}\quad
	\liminf_{R\to\infty}
	R^{-\gamma_{\mathrm{reg}}}
	\inf_{c\in\mathbb R}
	\|u-c\|_{\underline L^2(B_R)}=0\,,
\end{equation*}
then~$u$ is constant.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theoremA}

The energy estimate prevents solutions from concentrating their energy on too few small cubes. If the energy in~$\cu_m$ is positive, then a family of cubes~$z+\cu_n$, with~$n$ and~$z$ as in~\eqref{e.intro.large.scale.energy}, carrying a fixed positive proportion of that energy must contain at least a positive multiple of~$3^{(d-2(1-\gamma))(m-n)}$ cubes. The multiple depends on~$d$ and on the prescribed proportion. For~$\gamma=\gamma_{\mathrm{reg}}$, the exponent approaches~$d$ as~$\gmcdelta\to0$.

\smallskip

This control of energy on small cubes is used both to obtain subsequential limits and to prove that they coincide. Theorem~\ref{t.C} is proved in Section~\ref{s.Holder.regularity}, as Theorem~\ref{t.large.scale.Holder.multifractal}.

\subsection{Comparison with previous work}
\label{ss.lit.review}

Diffusion in random force fields was studied in the physics literature by~\citet{Fisher},~\citet*{KLY},~\citet*{FFQSS} and~\citet*{BCGLD}; see~\citet*{BG} for a review. For gradient drifts with log-correlated Gaussian potentials, renormalization group computations predicted subdiffusion with an exponent depending on the disorder strength~\citep[Section~4.3.2.3]{BG}.

\smallskip

In our normalization, the Gaussian potential has increment variance asymptotic to~$4\tau^2\log|x-y|/\log3$ as~$|x-y|\to\infty$. The weak-disorder formula of~\citet*[equation~(3)]{CastilloLeDoussal} therefore predicts a time of order~$r^{2+2\tau^2/(d\log3)}$ to travel distance~$r$. Estimate~\eqref{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic} gives this leading exponent for the time scale~$3^{2N}/\ahom_N$. In dimension two, the identity~$\ahom_N=e^{-(N+1)\tau^2}$ gives exactly the predicted exponent~$2+\tau^2/\log3$.

\smallskip

The planar lattice analogue is a random walk whose edge conductances are exponentials of the discrete Gaussian free field. For this model,~\citet*{BDG} proved recurrence, return-probability estimates and exponents for expected exit times. At small disorder,~\citet*{DW} proved tightness of the random quenched path laws for a version using a smooth approximation to the field.

\smallskip

In these walks, the probability of choosing a neighbor is proportional to the conductance of the joining edge, and the reversible measure at a vertex is the sum of the incident conductances. Thus the field affects both the transition probabilities and the reversible measure, as it does in~\eqref{e.sde.intro}.

\smallskip

For the walk of~\citet{BDG},~\citet*[Problem~16.13]{BiskupSurvey} formulated the scaling-limit problem through a continuum diffusion driven by the gradient of a smoothed planar Gaussian free field. The question asks for joint convergence of the field and the diffusion, after a suitable change of time, with continuous, nonconstant limiting paths. We prove existence and uniqueness of the scaling limit for the continuum model~\eqref{e.sde.intro} under~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}, at weak disorder and in every dimension~$d\geq2$. Identifying it with a scaling limit of either lattice model remains a separate question.

\smallskip

For Gaussian fields, the limiting reversible measure is obtained from Gaussian multiplicative chaos, introduced by~\citet{Kahane}. We use standard facts from this theory; see~\citet*{RVsurvey} and~\citet{Berestycki1} for accounts of the construction, and~\citet*{DRSV} for log-correlated fields.

\smallskip

Liouville Brownian motion, constructed by~\citet*{GRV} and~\citet{Berestycki2}, uses the chaos measure of the planar Gaussian free field to change the time parametrization of Brownian motion. Its Dirichlet form is the unweighted energy~$\int|\nabla u|^2\,dx$ on the~$L^2$ space of that measure. For a smooth positive density~$\a$, its generator is~$\a^{-1}\Delta$.

\smallskip

Our diffusion has energy~$\int\a|\nabla u|^2\,dx$ on~$L^2(\a\,dx)$ and generator~$\a^{-1}\nabla\cdot(\a\nabla)$. The field enters the energy as well as the reversible measure. Identifying the limit of these weighted energies is the main step in constructing our limiting process.

\smallskip

The renormalization argument is closely related to~\citet*{ASD}, who proved power-law superdiffusion in a self-similar incompressible random drift with sufficiently small positive Hurst exponent. Both papers follow an induction across scales for the elliptic operator, using variationally defined coarse-grained matrices in place of pointwise ellipticity bounds. In the incompressible model the effective diffusivity increases with scale; here it decreases. The present paper also constructs a scaling limit.

\subsection{Overview of the proofs}

We next outline the proof. The argument has three main parts; the central step is the identification of a unique limiting energy.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The coarse-graining estimates and the induction across scales give control of the effective diffusivities and large-scale regularity (Sections~\ref{s.coarse.graining}--\ref{s.Holder.regularity}).
\item We use this regularity to obtain subsequential limits of the energies, compare any two such limits, and show that they coincide (Section~\ref{sec:mfd-uniqueness}).
\item The limiting energy and reversible measure determine the diffusion. We prove convergence of the path laws and then derive the properties of the limit (Sections~\ref{sec:mfd-process-convergence}--\ref{lim:sec-process-measure}).
\end{enumerate}
The dependence of the main results is shown in Figure~\ref{f.dependence}. The appendices collect moment bounds, concentration inequalities and comparisons of Besov norms.

\subsubsection{Coarse-grained matrices}

Pointwise ellipticity deteriorates as the cutoff grows. Following~\citet*{AK.HC} and~\citet{ASD}, we work with the two \textbf{coarse-grained matrices} of a cube. One records the minimal energy for affine boundary values. The inverse of the other records the energy of the Neumann solution with the boundary flux of a constant vector field. Both equal the coefficient when it is constant.

\smallskip

Their difference measures the error in approximating the equation by a constant-coefficient equation. The matrices on subcubes also control Poincar\'e and Caccioppoli inequalities and harmonic approximation. Section~\ref{s.coarse.graining} recalls this deterministic theory. Normalizing the matrices by the effective diffusivity gives estimates that remain useful as that diffusivity tends to zero.

\subsubsection{Induction across scales and the effective diffusivities}

We prove that both coarse-grained matrices of~$\a_m$ on~$\cu_m$ stay close to~$\ahom_m\Id$. More precisely, their relative error is of order~$\gmcdelta$ in~$L^p(\P)$, uniformly in~$m$, for each fixed~$p<\infty$ and~$\gmcdelta$ sufficiently small depending on~$p$ (Proposition~\ref{p.coarse.grained.bound}).

\smallskip

The induction has two operations. First, we keep the cutoff~$L$ fixed and pass from cubes of side~$3^L$ to a larger cube~$\cu_m$. The restrictions of~$\a_L$ to well-separated subcubes are independent, so averaging reduces the gap between the coarse-grained matrices. As in~\citet*{AKMBook}, the variational estimate has two terms: spatial averages of the gradient of an optimizer, controlled using independence, and the decrease of the expected gap between scales. These decreases have a bounded sum. The estimates of~\citet{AK.HC} control both terms using coarse-grained ellipticity.

\smallskip

Second, we add~$\g_{L+1},\ldots,\g_m$. These fields need not be nearly constant on~$\cu_m$, but their effect is a small relative perturbation at weak disorder. Fields much coarser than a subcube are nearly constant there and multiply both coarse-grained matrices by nearly the same factor. We choose~$m-L$ so that the contraction from averaging absorbs the error from the added fields. Independence and the scaling law~\eqref{e.same.law.shift} then let us repeat the argument with the same constants.

\smallskip

The same induction lets us follow the effective diffusivity. To first order, adding a block of fields changes the variational optimizer by the solution of a Poisson equation forced by those fields. Its energy gives the leading decrease~$2\tau^2/d$ per scale in the logarithm of the diffusivity. Summing over blocks yields~\eqref{e.intro.diffusivity.asymptotic}; planar duality gives the exact formula in dimension two.

\smallskip

Power-law decay requires a separate argument, since the perturbative error need not be smaller than the leading term for every law satisfying~\ref{a.g1}--\ref{a.g4}. Proposition~\ref{p.homogenized.coefficient.strict.decay} proves this decay without weak disorder. Since~$\tau^2>0$, one field lowers the expected minimal energy with affine boundary values below the energy of the affine function by a fixed factor smaller than one. Such factors multiply over scales separated by a fixed gap.

\subsubsection{Large-scale regularity and homogenization}

The regularity argument compares solutions with harmonic functions. When the coarse-grained matrices are close to a constant matrix, a solution is close to a harmonic function and hence to an affine function on a smaller cube. Its error of affine approximation decreases across such a scale. On the remaining scales the error can increase, but only by a bounded factor.

\smallskip

The difficulty is that approximation errors on different scales depend on the same fields. We define a scale to be \textbf{good} by bounds on the fields and on the variational energies, with tolerances growing geometrically with the distance from that scale. Independence then shows that only a small proportion of scales fail these bounds, outside an event of exponentially small probability. The improvement on good scales absorbs the losses on the others. This gives both the H\"older exponent in Theorem~\ref{t.C} and the tail estimate~\eqref{e.intro.regularity.minimal.scale}.

\smallskip

Above the cutoff scale, no new fields enter, and averaging continues to improve the approximation. The estimates are therefore uniform in the cutoff. Combined with Caccioppoli's inequality,~\eqref{e.intro.large.scale.Holder} shows that small cubes, and in particular thin strips along their faces, carry little energy. We use this repeatedly in the compactness and uniqueness arguments. Theorem~\ref{t.B} follows directly from the coarse-graining estimates on the whole domain.

\subsubsection{Uniqueness of the limit form}

For the rescaled process, write~$\mu_N$ for the reversible measure and~$\int A_N|\nabla u|^2\,dx$ for its Dirichlet form on~$L^2(\mu_N)$. The coefficient~$A_N$ is the density of~$\mu_N$ divided by~$\ahom_N$. Apart from a positive factor from the fields at scales larger than one, independent of~$N$, it has the law of~$\ahom_N^{-1}\a_N(3^N\,\cdot)$. Thus~$N$ is the cutoff in these coordinates. The energy measure distributes a function's energy over space; before taking the limit it is~$A_N|\nabla u|^2\,dx$.

\smallskip

The measures~$\mu_N$ converge by the multiplicative chaos construction of~\citet{Kahane}. After removing the factor from the fields at scales larger than one, their masses on a fixed cube form a nonnegative martingale: each new field multiplies the density by an independent factor of mean one. At weak disorder these masses have a bounded moment of order greater than one, so no mass is lost. We must identify the limit of the energies.

\smallskip

Compactness comes from resampling. A field at a much smaller scale than a cube has little effect on its minimal energy. We divide the cube into boxes and remove this field from thin strips along their faces. The remaining parts of the boxes are farther apart than its range of dependence. Their restrictions of the field are independent, and the Efron--Stein inequality bounds the fluctuations of the modified energy. Regularity controls both this bound and the energy discarded in the strips.

\smallskip

Very coarse fields are nearly constant on the cube. Thus each minimal energy is close, uniformly in the cutoff, to its conditional expectation given finitely many fields. These conditional expectations form a compact family in~$L^p(\P)$ (Section~\ref{mfd:sec-resampling}). Limits of the Dirichlet Green operators then define random Dirichlet forms.

\smallskip

Let~$E$ and~$F$ be two subsequential limit forms. We first prove that they bound each other and have the same domain (Theorem~\ref{mfd:thm-C0}). After replacing fields coarser than a small cube by their values at its center, rescaling the cube multiplies both forms by the same random factor. Their deterministic normalizations can still differ. If their ratio were too small on a positive proportion of scales, harmonic replacement on cubes of those scales would make one energy too small, contradicting the positive and negative moment bounds. Interchanging the forms excludes large ratios. This adapts~\citet*[Section~3.4]{DGComparison}.

\smallskip

We next consider the optimal lower and upper comparison constants. Resampling one field multiplies both energy measures by the same weight and leaves the constants unchanged. Independence of the fields makes these constants deterministic.

\smallskip

If the constants differ, we improve one of them. On a small cube with affine boundary values, the minimal~$F$-energy is close to a deterministic multiple of the minimal~$E$-energy. This multiple lies between the constants and may depend on the scale. On a positive proportion of scales it lies on the same side of their midpoint.

\smallskip

Suppose it lies below the midpoint. On those scales, the minimal~$F$-energy for affine boundary values falls below the upper bound by a fixed fraction of the gap between the constants. The upper constant times the minimal~$E$-energy minus the minimal~$F$-energy is a nonnegative quadratic form in the boundary values, bounded by the gap times the minimal~$E$-energy. Consequently, the same improvement holds, with a controlled error, for boundary values close to affine.

\smallskip

Replacing an~$E$-harmonic function by~$F$-harmonic functions on suitable cubes and using lower semicontinuity improves the upper comparison constant. This contradicts its optimality. If the multiple lies above the midpoint, we interchange the forms. Thus the forms are proportional (Theorem~\ref{mfd:thm-prop}).

\smallskip

Two estimates make this argument work. Every error must be small relative to the gap between the comparison constants. We therefore apply Efron--Stein directly to a difference of the two minimal energies. Lemma~\ref{mfd:lem-diff} bounds the energy of the difference of the minimizers by a multiple of the squared gap times the minimal~$E$-energy. This gives the required concentration estimate (Proposition~\ref{mfd:prop-conc}).

\smallskip

The replacement cubes must also carry a fixed fraction of the solution's energy. The energy and the events on which regularity holds depend on the same fields. We control this dependence by bounding the number of failed scales along every chain of nested cubes, on one event valid for all solutions. Integrating against a solution's energy measure then supplies enough cubes for the replacements. The estimates required on each cube are collected in Lemma~\ref{mfd:in-deterministic}.

\smallskip

Finally, the proportionality factor is fixed by homogenization. Once the factor from the fields at scales larger than one is removed,~$A_N$ is stationary with homogenized matrix equal to the identity. Its coarse-grained matrices approach the identity on large cubes uniformly in~$N$ (Theorem~\ref{mfd:thm-eta}). Both limits inherit this normalization, so the proportionality factor is one (Theorem~\ref{mfd:thm-c1}).

\smallskip

The comparison of optimal constants follows~\citet*[Theorem~1.9]{GM}, who proved uniqueness up to a deterministic factor for weak subcritical Liouville quantum gravity metrics. In their argument, a shortest path corresponds to our minimizer, path length to energy, and replacement of a path segment to harmonic replacement on a cube.

\smallskip

They controlled local events along a shortest path through confluence of geodesics~\citep[Section~4]{GM}: geodesics from a common point share an initial segment. For critical and supercritical metrics,~\citet*[Section~4.2]{DGUniqueness} used modifications of the field in annuli. Our estimate along chains of cubes serves this purpose for energy measures and works in every dimension~$d\geq2$. We also prove the properties needed for the comparison: locality, consistency under restriction to subcubes, and compatibility with multiplication of the coefficient by a positive continuous function.

\subsubsection{Construction of the limiting diffusion}

The unique limit form and the limiting reversible measure~$\mu$ determine the limiting diffusion. To prove convergence, we begin with resolvents of the processes killed on leaving a cube. Although the densities of~$\mu_N$ have no uniform bound, their masses on balls are bounded by a random constant times a power of the radius, with exponent approaching~$d$ from below as the disorder strength decreases. These estimates are uniform in~$N$ and control integrals against~$\mu_N$ by energy.

\smallskip

We can therefore pass to the limit in the variational problems for the killed resolvents. Smoothing their measure-valued sources and applying the regularity estimates gives convergence uniformly in the starting point.

\smallskip

Tightness follows from bounds on the probability of leaving a small cube in a short time. We obtain them from a resolvent estimate for the Laplace transform of the exit time and a local maximum principle with respect to the reversible measure. The latter follows from Moser iteration (Section~\ref{s.tightness}). The same estimates confine paths to a large cube over bounded time intervals with high probability.

\smallskip

This confinement lets us pass from killed to whole-space resolvents. Iterating the resolvents determines the joint laws at finitely many times. Together with tightness, this gives convergence in probability on continuous path space, locally uniformly in the starting point.

\smallskip

For almost-sure convergence, we compare two finite cutoffs. We adapt the comparison argument of~\citet{DGComparison}, in the form strengthened by~\citet*[Lemma~3.11]{Devlin2024}. If the ratio of the deterministic energy normalizations stayed below one by a fixed amount on a positive proportion of scales, harmonic replacements on disjoint cubes would force one energy below its positive limit. This contradicts convergence in probability.

\smallskip

Excluding these ratios, and interchanging the cutoffs, gives comparisons with summable error probabilities. Borel--Cantelli yields almost-sure convergence of the energies. Bounds uniform in the cutoff then transfer it to the killed resolvents and the path laws.

\smallskip

The properties of the limiting paths follow from power-law decay of the effective diffusivities between arbitrary scales. This gives rapid exit from small cubes and hence singularity with respect to Brownian motion, both quenched and annealed.

\smallskip

Singularity of~$\mu$ follows from the usual change of measure in multiplicative chaos. Weighting the joint law of the field and a point by the stationary chaos measure exponentially tilts the fields at that point. The strong law shows that the approximating densities diverge at~$\mu$-almost every point and vanish at Lebesgue-almost every point. Finally, the transition laws are absolutely continuous with respect to~$\mu$. Since~$\mu$ is singular and charges no hyperplane, these laws cannot be Gaussian.

\subsection*{Use of artificial intelligence}

The authors developed all the main ideas of this paper in 2024. Versions of GPT were used to check the arguments, and Claude was used to help with the writing. The authors take responsibility for the mathematical content and presentation of the paper.

\section{Coarse-graining theory}\label{s.coarse.graining}

In this section we recall a deterministic coarse-graining theory for uniformly elliptic coefficient fields~$\a:\Rd\to\Rsymp$.  The two coarse-grained matrices of a bounded domain give two-sided bounds on the energy of solutions in that domain, and their difference measures how far the equation is from being homogenized. The main argument of this paper tracks this difference from one scale to the next.

\smallskip

Throughout this section, and in contrast to those that follow, no probabilistic structure is used: the coefficient field is arbitrary and fixed.  We define the matrices and record their subadditivity under partitions of a cube, show that solutions satisfy Poincar\'e and Caccioppoli inequalities with constants given by the coarse-grained matrices, estimate how the matrices change under a perturbation of the coefficient field, and combine these into the general coarse-graining estimate used later.  The definitions and estimates are from~\citet{AK.HC}, arXiv version~3, and~\citet{ASD}, arXiv version~2. We indicate below where a result is adapted to the present setting.

\subsection{Notation}
\label{s.notation}

For a matrix~$A$, the symbol~$|A|$ denotes its Euclidean operator norm,~$|A|=\sup_{p\neq0}|Ap|_2/|p|_2$. In quadratic and variational formulas we write~$|p|$ for~$|p|_2$.  We identify a positive scalar coefficient with the corresponding scalar multiple of~$\Id$. For the coarse-grained matrices~$\a(U)$ and~$\a_*(U)$ defined in~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.matrices}, superscripts denote matrix powers; for example,~$\a_*^{-1}(U)=(\a_*(U))^{-1}$. A coefficient field used to construct a coarse-grained matrix is written after a semicolon, as in~$\a(U;\a^{-1})$.

\smallskip

For a permutation~$\pi$ of~$\{1,\ldots,d\}$,~$n\in\Z$, and~$z\in\Rd$, define
\begin{equation*}
	\spx_n^\pi(z)  \coloneqq  z + 3^n  \Bigl\{ (x_1,\ldots,x_d) \in\Rd \,:\, -\nf12 < x_{\pi(1)} < x_{\pi(2)} < \cdots < x_{\pi(d)} < \nf12  \Bigr\}
	\,.
\end{equation*}
We call~$\spx_n^\pi(z)$ a \textbf{Kuhn simplex} of the cube~$z+\cu_n$; these~$d!$ simplices form its \textbf{Kuhn triangulation}.

\smallskip

For~$n\leq m$, each Kuhn simplex of a cube of side~$3^m$ is partitioned, up to null sets, by the scale-$n$ Kuhn simplices contained in it. We retain these fine simplices even when their supporting cubes cross the diagonal boundary of the parent simplex. Moreover, the cube~$z+\cu_n$ is the union of the simplices~$\spx_n^\pi(z)$ over all permutations~$\pi$, up to null sets, and~$|\spx_n^\pi(z)|=3^{nd}/d!$.

\subsubsection*{Function spaces}
The coarse-graining estimates of this section are stated in volume-normalized norms on cubes. The positive-order seminorms measure the oscillation of a function at all scales up to the size of the cube, the negative-order norms measure a gradient or a flux through its averages against test functions or over subcubes, and the spaces of solenoidal fields at the end of this subsection enter the variational formulas for the coarse-grained matrices. For~$s\in (0,1)$ and~$p\in [1,\infty)$, the fractional Sobolev seminorm on the cube~$\cu_m$ is defined by
\begin{equation*}
	[  u ]_{\underline{W}^{s,p}(\cu_m)}
	\coloneqq
	\biggl(
	s
	\fint_{\cu_m} \int_{\cu_m}
	\frac{|u(x) - u(y)|^p}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}
	\, dx \, dy
	\biggr)^{\! \nf1p}
	\,.
\end{equation*}
In this fractional Sobolev seminorm, distance and vector magnitude are Euclidean. At~$s=1$, the seminorm is the classical first-order one,~$\|\nabla u\|_{\underline L^p(\cu_m)}$, with Euclidean gradient magnitude. For a bounded Lipschitz set~$U$, the same formulas define~$[\,\cdot\,]_{\underline W^{s,p}(U)}$. We set
\begin{equation*}
	\|u\|_{\underline W^{s,p}(U)}
	\coloneqq
	[u]_{\underline W^{s,p}(U)}+
	|U|^{-\nf{s}{d}}\|u\|_{\underline L^p(U)}\,.
\end{equation*}
For~$s\in(0,1]$,~$q\in(1,\infty)$,~$N\in\N$, and~$F:U\to\R^N$, define the inhomogeneous volume-normalized negative Sobolev norm by
\begin{equation*}
	\|F\|_{\Wminusul{-s}{q}(U)}
	\coloneqq
	\sup_{\varphi\in C^\infty(\overline U;\R^N),\,\varphi\neq0}
	\frac{\left|\fint_U F\cdot\varphi\right|}
	{[\varphi]_{\underline W^{s,q'}(U)}+
	|U|^{-\nf{s}{d}}\|\varphi\|_{\underline L^{q'}(U)}}\,.
\end{equation*}
When~$q=2$ and~$N=d$, we write
\begin{equation}
	\|F\|_{\Hminusuls{-s}(U)}
	\coloneqq
	\sup_{\varphi\in C^\infty(\overline U;\Rd),\,\varphi\neq0}
	\frac{\left|\fint_U F\cdot\varphi\right|}
	{[\varphi]_{\underline H^s(U)}+
	|U|^{-\nf{s}{d}}\|\varphi\|_{\underline L^2(U)}}\,.
	\label{e.hatted.negative.norm.convention}
\end{equation}
Here and below,~$\underline H^s=\underline W^{s,2}$.  If~$F\in(H^s(U;\Rd))^*$, then the numerator in~\eqref{e.hatted.negative.norm.convention} is interpreted as~$|U|^{-1}|\langle F,\varphi\rangle|$; by density of smooth functions, the supremum is then the corresponding dual norm. For the unadorned negative norm in Theorem~\ref{t.B} we use zero-boundary tests:
\begin{equation}
	\|F\|_{\underline H^{-1}(U)}
	\coloneqq
	\sum_{i=1}^d
	\sup_{\substack{\varphi\in C_c^\infty(U)\\ \|\nabla\varphi\|_{\underline L^2(U;\ell^2)}\leq1}}
	\left|\fint_U F_i\varphi\right|\,.
	\label{e.ordinary.negative.norm.convention}
\end{equation}
Each summand is the volume-normalized norm of the component~$F_i$ in the dual of~$H^1_0(U)$. The hatted negative norm continues to use the unrestricted smooth tests of~\eqref{e.hatted.negative.norm.convention}. On~$\cu_0$ the volume-normalized and unnormalized conventions coincide. For every nonempty finite set~$A$, write~$\avsum_{z\in A}c_z\coloneqq\frac1{\#A}\sum_{z\in A}c_z$. In particular, if a cube~$U$ is the disjoint union of equal-volume cubes~$(Q_z)_{z\in A}$, then
\begin{equation}
	\avsum_{z\in A}
	\|f\|_{\underline L^p(Q_z)}^p
	=
	\|f\|_{\underline L^p(U)}^p\,.
	\label{e.average.normalized.norm.partition}
\end{equation}
We work with the (volume-normalized) Besov-type seminorm defined in the cube~$\cu_m$ for exponents~$s \in (0,1)$ and~$p,q,r\in [1,\infty)$ by
\begin{equation}
	[  u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{p,q,r}(\cu_m)}
	\coloneqq
	\biggl(
	s
	\sum_{n=-\infty}^m
	3^{-nsq}
	\biggl(
	\avsum_{z\in 3^{n-1} \Zd \cap \cu_m, \, z+\cu_n \subseteq \cu_m}
	\bigl\| u - (u)_{z+\cu_n} \bigr\|_{\underline{L}^p(z+\cu_n)}^r
	\biggr)^{\!\nf qr} \biggr)^{\!\nf1q} \,.
	\label{e.Besov.Morrey.seminorm}
\end{equation}
For~$r=p$, the seminorm reads
\begin{equation*}
	[  u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{p,q,p}(\cu_m)}
	=
	\biggl(
	s \sum_{n=-\infty}^m
	3^{-nsq}
	\biggl(
	\avsum_{z\in 3^{n-1} \Zd \cap \cu_m, \, z+\cu_n \subseteq \cu_m}
	\bigl\| u - (u)_{z+\cu_n} \bigr\|_{\underline{L}^p(z+\cu_n)}^p
	\biggr)^{\!\nf qp} \biggr)^{\!\nf1q} \,.
\end{equation*}
When the third index is omitted, it equals the first: we write~$\underline B^s_{p,q}\coloneqq\underline B^s_{p,q,p}$. The corresponding norm is~$\|f\|_{\underline B^s_{p,q,r}(\cu_m)} \coloneqq[f]_{\underline B^s_{p,q,r}(\cu_m)} +3^{-sm}|(f)_{\cu_m}|$. By Lemma~\ref{l.Wsp.vs.Bspp}, there exists~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, for every~$s\in(0,1)$ and~$p\in[1,\infty)$,
\begin{equation}
	C^{-1} [  u ]_{\underline{W}^{s,p}(\cu_m)}
	\leq
	[ u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{p,p,p}(\cu_m)}
	\leq
	C [  u ]_{\underline{W}^{s,p}(\cu_m)}
	\,.
	\label{e.Wsp.vs.Bspp.intro}
\end{equation}
The corresponding norms are equivalent for each fixed~$s,p$; unlike the seminorm comparison, their constants need not be uniform in these parameters.

\smallskip

We allow each of~$p,q,r$ to equal~$\infty$.  If~$p=\infty$, the volume-normalized~$L^p$ norm is replaced by the~$L^\infty$ norm; if~$r=\infty$, the average over cube centers is replaced by a supremum; and if~$q=\infty$, the sum over scales is replaced by a supremum.  We also allow~$s=1$ when~$q=\infty$.

\smallskip

We use Euclidean distance and vector magnitude in the H\"older seminorms. When~$q=r=\infty$, the Besov seminorm is equivalent to the H\"older seminorm of order~$s$ for every~$p$. More precisely,
\begin{align}
	C^{-1}s \sup_{x,y\in \cu_m, \, x\neq y}
	\frac{|u(x) - u(y)| }{|x-y|^s }
	&\leq
	[  u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{1,\infty,\infty}(\cu_m)}
	\leq
	[  u ]_{\underline{B}^s_{\infty,\infty,\infty}(\cu_m)}
	\notag \\ &
	\leq
	C \sup_{x,y\in \cu_m, \, x\neq y}
	\frac{|u(x) - u(y)| }{|x-y|^s }\,.
	\label{e.Besov.to.Holder}
\end{align}
The first inequality of~\eqref{e.Besov.to.Holder} is the only nontrivial one, and it is the Campanato characterization of H\"older spaces.

\smallskip

For~$s\in(0,1]$ and~$q,r\in[1,\infty]$, define
\begin{equation}
	\| f \|_{\Besov{-s}{r}{q}(\cu_m)}
	\coloneqq
	\biggl(
	s\sum_{n=-\infty}^m
	3^{nsq}
	\biggl(
	\avsum_{z\in 3^{n} \Zd \cap \cu_m}
	\bigl| (f)_{z+\cu_n} \bigr|^r
	\biggr)^{\!\nf qr} \biggr)^{\!\nf1q} \,.
	\label{e.Besov.weaknorm.def}
\end{equation}
If~$q^{-1}+(q')^{-1}=r^{-1}+(r')^{-1}=1$ and either~$s<1$ or~$(s,q)=(1,1)$, then Lemma~\ref{l.circ.norm.dominates} gives, for every~$f,g$ for which the right side is finite and~$fg\in L^1(\cu_m)$,
\begin{equation}
	\biggl| \fint_{\cu_m} f g \biggr|
	\leq
	C s^{-1}  \| g \|_{\underline{B}^{s}_{1,q',r'}(\cu_m)}
	\| f \|_{\Besov{-s}{r}{q}(\cu_m)}\,.
	\label{e.duality.splitting}
\end{equation}
For a bounded convex domain~$U\subseteq\Rd$, set
\begin{equation}
	L^2_{\sol}(U)\coloneqq\bigl(\nabla H^1_0(U)\bigr)^\perp
	\qquad\text{and}\qquad
	\Lsolo(U)\coloneqq\bigl(\nabla H^1(U)\bigr)^\perp\,.
	\label{e.sols.and.pots}
\end{equation}
All orthogonal complements in~\eqref{e.sols.and.pots} are taken in~$L^2(U;\Rd)$.

\subsection{Coarse-grained matrices}
\label{ss.cg.mats}

We denote the linear space of solutions~$u$ of~$-\nabla \cdot \a \nabla u = 0$ in an open set~$U \subseteq \Rd$ by
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{A}(U; \a)  \coloneqq  \{ u \in H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(U) : -\nabla \cdot \a \nabla u = 0 \quad \text{in $U$}\}
	\,.
\end{equation*}
For every~$p, q \in \Rd$ and bounded convex domain~$U\subseteq\Rd$, we define the quantity
\begin{equation}
	J(U,p,q; \a)  \coloneqq  \sup_{v \in \mathcal{A}(U;\a)\cap H^1(U)} \fint_{U} \Bigl( -\frac{1}{2} \nabla v \cdot \a \nabla v - p \cdot \a \nabla v + q \cdot \nabla v \Bigr)
	\,.
	\label{e.J.general}
\end{equation}
The supremum is attained in~$H^1(U)$, and the maximizer is unique up to an additive constant. We denote its mean-zero representative by~$v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a)$, so that~$(v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a))_U=0$. For a bounded convex domain~$U \subseteq \Rd$, define the~\textbf{coarse-grained matrices}~$\a(U)$ and~$\a_*(U)$ by the identity, valid for every~$p,q\in\Rd$,
\begin{equation}
	J(U, p,q; \a) \eqqcolon \frac{1}{2} p \cdot \a(U) p + \frac{1}{2} q \cdot \a_{*}^{-1}(U) q - p \cdot q \,.
	\label{e.coarse.grained.matrices}
\end{equation}
The identity~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.matrices} records both matrices in one quadratic function of~$(p,q)$, which is subadditive under partitions of~$U$ (see~\eqref{e.subadditivity.J} below). The matrices also have variational characterizations~\citep[Lemma~10.5]{AKMBook}, using the \href{https://perso.ens-lyon.fr/jean-christophe.mourrat/AKMbook.pdf}{version dated 7~May~2019}, p.~384, which express them as minimal energies; we use these to compare the coarse-grained matrices of two coefficient fields in Section~\ref{ss.sensitivity}. For~$p, q \in \Rd$,
\begin{equation}
	\frac12 p \cdot \a(U) p  = \min_{u \in p\cdot x + H_0^1(U)} \fint_{U}\frac12 \nabla u \cdot \a \nabla u
	=  \max_{\g \in \Lsol(U)} \fint_U \Bigl(-\frac12 \g \cdot \a^{-1} \g + p \cdot \g  \Bigr) \,,
	\label{e.variational.a}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
	\frac12 q \cdot \a^{-1}_*(U) q
	=
	\max_{w \in H^1(U)} \fint_{U} \Bigl(-\frac12 \nabla w \cdot \a \nabla w + q \cdot \nabla w \Bigr)
	= \min_{\g \in q + \Lsolo(U)} \fint_{U} \frac12 \g \cdot \a^{-1} \g \,.
	\label{e.variational.astar}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Properties of the coarse-grained matrices}

We use the following identities and inequalities; see~\citet{AK.Book,AK.HC}.  Throughout,~$U\subseteq\Rd$ is a bounded convex domain.

\smallskip

The coarse-grained matrices are bounded by averages of the field, in the Loewner order:
\begin{equation}
	\Bigl( \fint_{U} \a^{-1} \Bigr)^{\!-1} \leq \a_{*}(U) \leq \a(U)
	\leq
	\fint_{U} \a  \,.
	\label{e.ord.bounds.truncated}
\end{equation}

\smallskip

The coarse-grained matrices are~\textbf{subadditive}: for every partition~$\{ U_i \}_{i=1}^N$ of~$U$ (up to a Lebesgue null set)
\begin{equation}
	\a(U) \leq  \sum_{i=1}^N \frac{|U_i|}{|U|} \a(U_i)  \qand \a_*(U) \geq \biggl( \sum_{i=1}^N \frac{|U_i|}{|U|}\a^{-1}_*(U_i) \biggr)^{-1} \,,
	\label{e.subadditivity.a}
\end{equation}
which generalizes the first and third inequalities of~\eqref{e.ord.bounds.truncated}. Equivalently, for every~$p, q \in \Rd$,
\begin{equation}
	J(U, p, q; \a) \leq
	\sum_{i=1}^N \frac{|U_i|}{|U|} J(U_i, p , q; \a) \,.
	\label{e.subadditivity.J}
\end{equation}
The spatial averages of the gradient and the flux of the maximizer can be written explicitly in terms of the coarse-grained matrices: for~$p, q \in \Rd$,
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		& \fint_U
		\nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q; \a)
		=
		- p + \a_{*}^{-1}(U) q
		\\ &
		\fint_U
		\a \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q; \a)
		=
		q - \a(U) p\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
	\label{e.v.spatial.averages}
\end{equation}
For every~$e\in\Rd$, the maximizer~$v(\cdot,U,0,\a_{*}(U)e;\a)$ satisfies, by~\eqref{e.v.spatial.averages},
\begin{equation*}
	\begin{pmatrix}
		\bigl(\nabla v(\cdot,U,0,\a_{*}(U)e; \a) \bigr)_{U}  \\
		\bigl( \a   \nabla v(\cdot,U,0,\a_{*}(U)e; \a) \bigr)_{U}
	\end{pmatrix}
	=
	\begin{pmatrix}
		e  \\  \a_{*}(U)  e
	\end{pmatrix}
	\,.
\end{equation*}
The identity~\eqref{e.J.plug.identity} below gives~$J(U,e,\a_{*}(U)e\,;\a)=\frac12 e\cdot(\a(U)-\a_{*}(U))e$. For every~$w\in\A(U;\a)\cap H^1(U)$ and~$p,q\in\Rd$, the first variation of~\eqref{e.J.general} gives
\begin{align}
	q\cdot \fint_U \nabla w - p \cdot \fint_U \a \nabla w
	=
	\fint_U \nabla w \cdot \a \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q; \a)\,.
	\label{e.firstvar}
\end{align}
The second variation gives
\begin{align}
	& J(U,p,q; \a) - \fint_U \Bigl  ( -\frac12 \nabla w \cdot \a \nabla w -p\cdot \a \nabla w+ q\cdot \nabla w   \Bigr  )
	\notag \\ & \qquad \qquad\qquad
	=
	\fint_U \frac12 \bigl ( \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) - \nabla w \bigr )\cdot \a \bigl ( \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) - \nabla w \bigr )\,.
	\label{e.secondvar}
\end{align}
It follows that~$J$ can be written as the energy of the maximizer: for every~$p,q\in\Rd$,
\begin{equation}
	J(U,p,q;\a) = \fint_U \frac12 \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) \cdot \a \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a)
	\,.
	\label{e.Jenergy.v}
\end{equation}
The Euler equation also gives
\begin{equation*}
	J(U,p,q;\a) = \frac12 \Bigl( q\cdot \fint_U \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) - p \cdot \fint_U \a \nabla v(\cdot,U,p,q;\a) \Bigr) \,.
\end{equation*}
The coarse-grained matrix~$\a_{*}(U)$ bounds the energy of an arbitrary~$H^1(U)$ function from below in terms of the spatial average of its gradient: for every~$u\in H^1(U)$,
\begin{align}
	\frac12 \bigl(  \nabla u \bigr)_U \cdot \a_{*}(U) \bigl(  \nabla u \bigr)_U
	\leq
	\fint_U \frac12 \nabla u \cdot \a \nabla u
	\,.
	\label{e.energymaps}
\end{align}
The dual inequality is, for every~$\h\in L^2_{\sol}(U)$,
\begin{align*}
	\frac12\bigl( \h \bigr)_U \cdot \a^{-1} (U) \bigl( \h \bigr)_U
	\leq
	\fint_U \frac12 \h \cdot \a^{-1} \h
	\,.
\end{align*}
For every constant positive-definite matrix~$\b\in\Rsymp$ and every~$u\in\mathcal A(U;\a)\cap H^1(U)$,
\begin{equation}
	\bigl| \bigl(  ( \a - \b) \nabla u \bigr)_U
	\bigr|
	\leq
	2\biggl(\max_{|e|=1}J(U,e,\b e\,;\a)\biggr)^{\nf12}
	\biggl( \fint_U \frac12 \nabla u \cdot \a \nabla u  \biggr)^{\!\nf12}
	\,,
	\label{e.fluxmaps}
\end{equation}
in particular, with the coarse-graining error~$\mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,1}$ of Definition~\ref{d.mathcal.E} below, for every~$m \in \Z$ and every~$u\in\mathcal A(\cu_m;\a)\cap H^1(\cu_m)$,
\begin{equation*}
	3^{-s m} \| ( \a-\b) \nabla u \|_{\Besov{-s}{2}{1}(\cu_m)}
	\leq
	4s^{-1}
	|\b|^{\nf12}
	\|\a^{\nf12} \nabla u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_m)}
	\mathcal{E}_ {s,\infty,1} (\cu_m;\a,\b)
	\,.
\end{equation*}
The bound~\eqref{e.fluxmaps} follows from~\eqref{e.firstvar},~\eqref{e.Jenergy.v}, and the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality.

\smallskip

The identity~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.matrices} can be rewritten for every symmetric matrix~$\b \in \R^{d \times d}_{\sym}$ as
\begin{align}
	J(U,e,\b e; \a)
	&=
	\frac12 e \cdot \bigl( \a(U) - \a_{*}(U) \bigr) e
	+
	\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\b - \a_{*}(U)\bigr) \a_{*}^{-1}(U) \bigl(\b - \a_{*}(U)\bigr) e
	\,.
	\label{e.J.plug.identity}
\end{align}
If~$\b\in\Rsymp$, then completing the square in the first argument of~$J$ instead of the second gives
\begin{align}
	J(U,\b^{-1} e, e; \a)
	&
	=
	\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\a_{*}^{-1}(U) - \a^{-1}(U)\bigr)e
	+
	\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\b^{-1} - \a^{-1} (U)\bigr) \a(U) \bigl(\b^{-1} - \a^{-1} (U)\bigr) e
	\,.
	\label{e.J.plug.identity.inverses}
\end{align}
Applying~\eqref{e.J.plug.identity} and~\eqref{e.J.plug.identity.inverses} with~$\b^{-\nf12} e$ and~$\b^{\nf12} e$, respectively, in place of~$e$ yields
\begin{align}
	\label{e.J.half.half.identity}
	J(U,\b^{-\nf12}e,\b^{\nf12} e;\a)
	&
	=
	\frac12 e \cdot \b^{-\nf12}\bigl( \a(U) - \a_{*}(U) \bigr) \b^{-\nf12}e
	\notag \\ & \qquad
	+
	\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\Id - \b^{-\nf12}\a_{*}(U)\b^{-\nf12}\bigr) \b^{\nf12}\a_{*}^{-1}(U) \b^{\nf12} \bigl(\Id - \b^{-\nf12}\a_{*}(U)\b^{-\nf12}\bigr) e
	\\ &
	\label{e.J.half.half.identity.numbertwo}
	=
	\frac12 e \cdot \b^{\nf12}\bigl( \a_{*}^{-1}(U)- \a^{-1}(U)  \bigr) \b^{\nf12}e
	\notag \\ & \qquad
	+
	\frac12 e \cdot \bigl(\Id - \b^{\nf12}\a^{-1}(U)\b^{\nf12}\bigr) \b^{-\nf12}\a(U) \b^{-\nf12} \bigl(\Id - \b^{\nf12}\a^{-1}(U)\b^{\nf12}\bigr) e
	\,.
\end{align}

\begin{lemma}
\label{l.sharp.compare.J}
For every bounded convex domain~$U\subseteq\Rd$ and~$\b\in\Rsymp$,
\begin{align}
	\max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a)
	&
	\leq
	\frac12 \bigl|\b^{-\nf12} \bigl( \a(U) - \a_{*}(U) \bigr) \b^{-\nf12}  \bigr|
	+
	\frac12 \bigl| \b^{-\nf12} \a^{\nf12}_*(U) -  \b^{\nf12} \a^{-\nf12}_*(U)\bigr|^2
	\notag \\ &
	\leq
	2 \max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a)  \,,
	\label{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin}
\end{align}
and
\begin{align}
	\max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a)
	&
	\leq
	\frac12 \bigl|\b^{\nf12}   \bigl( \a^{-1}(U) - \a^{-1}_{*}(U) \bigr) \b^{\nf12}  \bigr|
	+
	\frac12 \bigl| \b^{\nf12} \a^{-\nf12}(U) -  \b^{-\nf12} \a^{\nf12}(U)\bigr|^2
	\notag \\ &
	\leq
	2 \max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a)  \,.
	\label{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin2}
\end{align}
In particular, there exists a universal constant~$C<\infty$ such that
\begin{multline}
	\bigl|\b^{-\nf12} \bigl( \a(U) - \a_{*}(U) \bigr) \b^{-\nf12} \bigr|
	+
	\bigl|\b^{\nf12} \a^{-1}_*(U) \b^{\nf12} - \Id \bigr|^2
	+
	\bigl|\b^{-\nf12} \a(U) \b^{-\nf12} - \Id\bigr|^2
	\\
	\leq
	C\max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a) \bigl(1 + \max_{|e| = 1} J(U, \b^{-\nf12}   e, \b^{\nf12}  e; \a) \bigr)  \,.
	\label{e.coarse.grained.matrix.deviation.by.J}
\end{multline}

\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For positive-semidefinite matrices~$M$ and~$N$, we have~$\max\{|M|,|N|\}\leq|M+N|\leq|M|+|N|\leq2|M+N|$. Applying this inequality to the two summands in~\eqref{e.J.half.half.identity} proves~\eqref{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin}; applying it to the two summands in~\eqref{e.J.half.half.identity.numbertwo} proves~\eqref{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin2}.

\smallskip

For every positive-definite matrix~$A$ and~$J\geq0$,
\begin{equation}
	\frac 12 \bigl| (\Id-A)A^{-1} (\Id-A) \bigr|
	\leq J
	\quad \implies \quad
	\bigl|\Id-A\bigr|^2 \leq 4J(1+J)\,.
	\label{e.from.a.ratios.to.J}
\end{equation}
Indeed, the hypothesis implies
\begin{equation*}
	\bigl| \Id - A \bigr|^2
	\leq
	2J|A|
	\leq
	2J|\Id - A | + 2J
	\leq
	\frac12 |\Id - A |^2 + 2J^2 + 2J\,.
\end{equation*}
Since~$(\Id-A)A^{-1}(\Id-A)=(\Id-A^{-1})A(\Id-A^{-1})$, apply~\eqref{e.from.a.ratios.to.J} with~$A=(\b^{-\nf12}\a_*(U)\b^{-\nf12})^{-1}$ in~\eqref{e.J.half.half.identity} and with~$A=(\b^{\nf12}\a^{-1}(U)\b^{\nf12})^{-1}$ in~\eqref{e.J.half.half.identity.numbertwo}. This gives
\begin{align}
	\lefteqn{
	\max\Bigl\{
	\bigl|\b^{-\nf12}\a(U)\b^{-\nf12} - \Id \bigr|^2
	\, ,\,
	\bigl|\b^{\nf12}\a_{*}^{-1}(U; \a)\b^{\nf12} - \Id \bigr|^2
	\Bigr\}
	} \qquad &
	\notag \\ &
	\leq
	10\sup_{|e|=1}
	J(U,\b^{-\nf12}e,\b^{\nf12} e; \a)
	\bigl( 1 + \sup_{|e|=1}
	J(U,\b^{-\nf12}e,\b^{\nf12} e; \a)  \bigr)
	\,.
	\label{e.aratios.to.J}
\end{align}
Combining~\eqref{e.aratios.to.J} with~\eqref{e.bound.for.J.general.preplugin} gives~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.matrix.deviation.by.J}.
\end{proof}

For every~$\lambda>0$, bounded convex domain~$U\subseteq\Rd$ and~$p,q\in\Rd$,
\begin{equation}
	J(U,p,q\,;\a) = J(U,\lambda^{-\nf12} p, \lambda^{\nf12}q \,; \lambda \a) \,.
	\label{e.homogeneous.J}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Coarse-grained elliptic and functional inequalities}
\label{ss.cg.ellipticity}

% HC v3, highcontrast-arxiv-v3.tex, e.coarse.grained.ellipticity (2.39).
Following~\citet[Definition~2.1, (2.39), p.~25]{AK.HC}, we attach to each cube~$\cu_m$ the coarse-grained ellipticity constants~$\lambda_{s,q}(\cu_m;\a)$ and~$\Lambda_{s,q}(\cu_m;\a)$. The resulting Poincar\'e and Caccioppoli inequalities depend on these constants rather than on the pointwise ellipticity ratio.

\smallskip

The weights~$(1-3^{-s})3^{-ns}$ sum to one over~$n\geq0$. For~$0<s\leq2$, their normalizing factor satisfies~$\frac49 s\leq1-3^{-s}\leq(\log3)s$.

\begin{definition}[Coarse-grained ellipticity constants]
For~$s>0$,~$q\in[1,\infty)$,~$m\in\Z$, and a uniformly elliptic field~$\a:\cu_m\to\Rsymp$, define the \textbf{coarse-grained ellipticity constants} by
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		& {\Lambda}_{s,q}(\cu_m\,;\a)
		\coloneqq
		\biggl(
		(1-3^{-sq}) \sum_{k=-\infty}^{m}
		3^{-sq(m-k)}
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd \cap \cu_m}
		\bigl| \a(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|^{\nf q2}
		\biggr)^{\!\nf{2}{q}}
		\,, \\  &
		{\lambda}_{s,q}(\cu_m;\a)
		\coloneqq
		\biggl((1-3^{-sq}) \sum_{k=-\infty}^{m}
		3^{-sq(m-k)}
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd \cap \cu_m}
		\bigl| \a_{*}^{-1}(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|^{\nf q2}
		\biggr)^{\!- \nf{2}{q}}
		\,,
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
	\label{e.coarse.grained.ellipticity}
\end{equation}
and, for~$q=\infty$,
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		& {\Lambda}_{s,\infty}(\cu_m\,;\a)
		\coloneqq
		\sup_{k\in(-\infty,m]\cap \Z}
		3^{-2s(m-k)}
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd \cap \cu_m}
		\bigl| \a(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|
		\,, \\  &
		{\lambda}_{s,\infty}(\cu_m;\a)
		\coloneqq
		\biggl(
		\sup_{k\in(-\infty,m]\cap \Z}
		3^{-2s(m-k)}
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd \cap \cu_m}
		\bigl| \a_{*}^{-1}(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|
		\biggr)^{\!-1}
		\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
	\label{e.coarse.grained.ellipticity.infty}
\end{equation}
When the exponent~$q$ is omitted, it is understood that~$q=1$, that is,~$\Lambda_{s}(\cu_m;\a)=\Lambda_{s,1}(\cu_m;\a)$ and~$\lambda_{s}(\cu_m;\a)=\lambda_{s,1}(\cu_m;\a)$. Equation~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.ellipticity.infty} is the limit of~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.ellipticity} as~$q\to\infty$.  On~$y+\cu_m$, every subcube~$z+\cu_k$ in these definitions is replaced by~$y+z+\cu_k$, with~$z\in3^k\Zd\cap\cu_m$.
\end{definition}
For every~$m\in\Z$,~$q\in [1,\infty]$ and~$t,s\in (0,\infty)$ with~$t<s$, we have
\begin{equation}
	\lambda_{t,q}(\cu_m; \a)
	\leq
	\lambda_{s,q} (\cu_m; \a)
	\leq |\a_*^{-1}(\cu_m;\a) |^{-1}
	\leq
	|\a(\cu_m;\a)|
	\leq
	\Lambda_{s,q}(\cu_m; \a)
	\leq
	\Lambda_{t,q}(\cu_m; \a)
	\,.
	\label{e.ellipticities.monotone.ordered}
\end{equation}
There exists~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, for every~$s\in(0,1]$ and~$p,q\in[1,\infty]$ with~$p\leq q$,
\begin{equation*}
	\Lambda_{s,q} (\cu_m;\a)
	\leq
	C s^{\nf2q - \nf2p}
	\Lambda_{s,p} (\cu_m;\a)
	\leq
	C s^{\nf2q - 2}
	\Lambda_{s} (\cu_m;\a)
	\,,
\end{equation*}
and
\begin{equation*}
	\lambda^{-1}_{s,q} (\cu_m;\a)
	\leq
	C s^{\nf2q - \nf2p}
	\lambda^{-1}_{s,p} (\cu_m;\a)
	\leq
	C s^{\nf2q -2}
	\lambda^{-1}_{s} (\cu_m;\a)
	\,.
\end{equation*}
For every~$k\in(-\infty,m]\cap\Z$, applying~\eqref{e.ellipticities.monotone.ordered} on the subcubes and comparing the definitions gives
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		&
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\bigl| \a(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|
		\leq
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\Lambda_{s,q} (z+\cu_k; \a)
		\leq 3^{2s(m-k)} \Lambda_{s,q} (\cu_m;\a)
		\,,
		\\ &
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\bigl| \a_{*}^{-1}(z+\cu_k; \a) \bigr|
		\leq
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\lambda_{s,q}^{-1} (z+\cu_k; \a)
		\leq 3^{2s(m-k)} \lambda_{s,q}^{-1} (\cu_m;\a)
		\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
	\label{e.bound.one.cube.by.lambdas}
\end{equation}

\smallskip

We measure the error in replacing~$\a$ by a constant matrix~$\a_0$ through the quantities~$J(z+\cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf12}e,\a_0^{\nf12}e;\a)$ on the subcubes~$z+\cu_l$.

\begin{definition}[Coarse-graining error]
\label{d.mathcal.E}
For~$s\in(0,1]$,~$p,q\in[1,\infty]$,~$m,n\in\Z$ with~$n\leq m$, a uniformly elliptic field~$\a:\cu_m\to\Rsymp$, and a matrix~$\a_0\in\Rsymp$, define the \textbf{coarse-graining error} by
\begin{align*}
	\lefteqn{
	\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (\cu_m , n; \a, \a_0 )
	} \ \notag \\ &
	=
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		&
		\biggl(
		(1-3^{-sq})
		\sum_{l=-\infty}^n
		3^{-sq(n-l)}
		\biggl(
		\avsum_{z \in 3^l\Zd \cap \cu_m}
		\max_{|e| =1} \bigl( J\bigl(z {+} \cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr) \bigr)^{\nf p2}
		\biggr)^{\!\nf qp}
		\biggr)^{\!\nf1q}
		\,,
		\quad  p,q<\infty\,,
		\\ &
		\biggl(
		(1-3^{-sq})
		\sum_{l=-\infty}^n
		3^{-sq(n-l)} \!\!
		\max_{z \in 3^l\Zd \cap \cu_m}
		\max_{|e| =1} \bigl( J\bigl(z {+} \cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr) \bigr)^{\nf q2}
		\biggr)^{\!\nf1q}
		\,,
		\quad p=\infty,\ q<\infty\,,
		\\
		&
		\sup_{l \in (-\infty,n]\cap \Z}
		3^{-s(n-l)}
		\biggl(
		\avsum_{z \in 3^l\Zd \cap \cu_m}
		\max_{|e| =1} \bigl( J\bigl(z {+} \cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr) \bigr)^{\nf p2}
		\biggr)^{\!\nf 1p}
		\,,
		\quad p<\infty,\ q=\infty \,,
		\\ &
		\sup_{l \in (-\infty,n]\cap \Z}
		3^{-s(n-l)}
		\max_{z \in 3^l\Zd \cap \cu_m}
		\max_{|e| =1} \bigl( J\bigl(z {+} \cu_l,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr) \bigr)^{\nf 12}
		\,,
		\quad p = q = \infty \,.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
\end{align*}
On~$y+\cu_m$, every subcube~$z+\cu_l$ in this definition is replaced by~$y+z+\cu_l$, with~$z\in3^l\Zd\cap\cu_m$.  We use the abbreviation
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (\cu_m ; \a, \a_0 )
	\coloneqq
	\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (\cu_m , m; \a, \a_0 )
	\,.
\end{equation*}
When the exponent~$q$ is omitted, it is understood that~$q=1$.
\end{definition}

The definition gives, for~$m\in\Z$,~$p\in[2,\infty]$,~$q\in[1,\infty]$, and~$0<t<s\leq1$,
\begin{equation*}
	\max_{|e| =1}  J\bigl(\cu_m,\a_0^{-\nf 12} e, \a_0^{\nf 12} e \,; \a\bigr)^{\nf12}
	\leq
	\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (\cu_m ; \a, \a_0 )
	\leq
	\mathcal{E}_{t,p,q} (\cu_m ; \a, \a_0 )
	\,,
\end{equation*}
and, for every~$k\in (-\infty,m]\cap \Z$,
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		&
		\max_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,q} (z+\cu_k;\a, \a_0)
		\leq 3^{s(m-k)} \mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,q}  (\cu_m;\a,\a_0)
		\,,
		\\ &
		\avsum_{z\in 3^k\Zd\cap \cu_m}
		\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q} (z+\cu_k;\a, \a_0)
		\leq 3^{(s+d(\frac1p-\frac1q)_+)(m-k)}
		\mathcal{E}_{s,p,q}  (\cu_m;\a,\a_0)
		\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
	\label{e.bound.one.cube.by.mathcalE}
\end{equation}

\smallskip

For a positive scalar~$\a_0$,~$s\in(0,1]$ and~$q\in[1,\infty]$,
\begin{align}
	\frac12 \mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,q}
	(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)^2
	&
	\leq
	\max\bigl\{
	|\a_0|^{-1} \Lambda_{s,q}(\cu_m;\a)
	\,,
	|\a_0| \lambda_{s,q}^{-1} (\cu_m;\a)
	\bigr\}
	\notag \\ &
	\leq
	\bigl(1+\sqrt2\,\mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,q}(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)\bigr)^2
	\,.
	\label{e.bound.Lambdas.by.Es}
\end{align}
To see the upper bound, put~$J_U=\max_{|e|=1}J(U,\a_0^{-\nf12}e,\a_0^{\nf12}e;\a)$. The ordering~$\a_*(U)\leq\a(U)$ and~\eqref{e.coarse.grained.matrices} imply that each eigenvalue~$t$ of~$\a_0^{-1}\a(U)$ or~$\a_0\a_*^{-1}(U)$ satisfies~$t+t^{-1}-2\leq2J_U$. Thus~$\sqrt t\leq1+\sqrt{2J_U}$ and~$t\leq1+2J_U+\sqrt{2J_U}$. The first inequality, followed by the weighted~$\ell^q$ triangle inequality, gives the upper bound in~\eqref{e.bound.Lambdas.by.Es}. For the lower bound, use~$\sqrt{J_U}\leq2^{-\nf12} (|\a_0^{-1}\a(U)|^{\nf12}+|\a_0\a_*^{-1}(U)|^{\nf12})$ and the same triangle inequality. The second eigenvalue bound also gives
\begin{equation*}
	\max\{\a_0^{-1}\Lambda_{s,2}(\cu_m;\a),\a_0\lambda_{s,2}^{-1}(\cu_m;\a)\}
	\leq1+2\mathcal E_{s,\infty,2}^2(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)+2\mathcal E_{s,\infty,2}(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)\,.
\end{equation*}
Moreover, in the case~$q = 2$,
\begin{equation*}
	1+\frac12 \mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,2}
	(\cu_m;\a,\a_0)^2
	\leq
	\max\bigl\{
	|\a_0|^{-1} \Lambda_{s,2}(\cu_m;\a)
	\,,
	|\a_0| \lambda_{s,2}^{-1} (\cu_m;\a)
	\bigr\} \,.
\end{equation*}
By Definition~\ref{d.mathcal.E}, for every~$p,q \in [1,\infty]$ and~$s \in (\nf{d}{p},1)$,
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{E}_{s, \infty, q} (\cu_m ; \a, \a_0 )
	\leq
	\Bigl(
	\frac{(1-3^{-sq})}{(1-3^{-q(s-\frac{d}{p})})}
	\Bigr)^{\nf1q}
	\mathcal{E}_{s - \nf{d}{p}, p, q}(\cu_m;\a,\a_0) \,.
\end{equation*}
For~$1\leq q\leq p<\infty$ and~$s>0$,
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{E}_{s, \infty, q}(\cu_m; \a, \a_0)
	\leq
	\mathcal{E}_{\nf{sq}{p}, \infty, p}(\cu_m; \a, \a_0)
	\,.
\end{equation*}
The same definitions give
\begin{equation}
	\Lambda_{s, q}(\cu_m; \a)
	\leq
	\Lambda_{\nf{sq}{p}, p}(\cu_m; \a)
	\qand
	\lambda_{s, q}^{-1}(\cu_m; \a)
	\leq
	\lambda_{\nf{sq}{p}, p}^{-1}(\cu_m; \a) \,.
	\label{e.compareLambdaqs}
\end{equation}
By H\"older's inequality, there exists a universal constant~$C<\infty$ such that, for~$0<s<t\leq1$,
\begin{equation}
	\mathcal{E}_{t,\infty,1}(\cu_m , n; \a, \a_0)
	\leq
	\frac{Ct}{(t-s)^{\nf12}\, s^{\nf12}} \, \mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,2}(\cu_m , n; \a, \a_0)
	\,.
	\label{e.mathcalE.one.vs.mathcalE.two}
\end{equation}

\smallskip

% ASD v2, algsuperdiff.tex, l.lambdas.stability (Lemma 2.5)
% and e.mathcalE.stability (2.62).
The coarse-grained ellipticity constants satisfy the following stability estimate, which is~\citet[Lemma~2.5, p.~33]{ASD}.

\begin{lemma}[Stability of the coarse-grained ellipticity constants]
\label{l.lambdas.stability}
There exists a constant~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, for every~$q\in[1,\infty]$,~$s,t\in(0,\nf12]$ with~$s<t$, and~$x\in\cu_0$ such that~$x+\cu_{-1}\subseteq\cu_0$, we have the estimates
\begin{equation}
	\lambda_{t,q}^{-1}(x+\cu_{-1}\,;\a)
	\leq
	\frac{C}{1-2s} \Bigl( \frac{t}{t-s}\Bigr)^{\! \nf 2q} \lambda_{s,q}^{-1}(\cu_{0}\,;\a) \,,
	\label{e.lambda.stability}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
	\Lambda_{t,q}(x+\cu_{-1}\,;\a)
	\leq
	\frac{C}{1-2s} \Bigl( \frac{t}{t-s}\Bigr)^{\! \nf 2q} \Lambda_{s,q}(\cu_{0}\,;\a)\,,
	\label{e.big.Lambda.stability}
\end{equation}
and, for every~$\a_0\in\Rsymp$ (see~\citet[(2.62), p.~33]{ASD}),
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{E}_{t,\infty,2}(x+\cu_{-1}\,;\a,\a_0)
	\leq
	\frac{C}{(1-2s)^{\nf12}} \Bigl( \frac{t}{t-s}\Bigr)^{\! \nf 12}
	\mathcal{E}_{s,\infty,2}(\cu_0\,;\a,\a_0)
	\,.
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}

% HC v3, highcontrast-arxiv-v3.tex, l.crude.weaknorms (Lemma 2.2);
% adapted: arbitrary H^1 gradients and L^2_sol fields.
The next proposition is the weak-norm estimate of~\citet[Lemma~2.2, p.~27]{AK.HC}, stated there for the gradient and flux of a solution. The same argument uses~\eqref{e.energymaps} and its dual on each subcube, so it applies to every~$u\in H^1(\cu_m)$ and every~$\h\in L^2_{\mathrm{sol}}(\cu_m)$. In the flux case, a zero-trace test function on a subcube extends by zero to one on~$\cu_m$, so the restriction of~$\h$ is still solenoidal. The positive-order estimate~\eqref{e.CG.Poincare.mean.zero} below corresponds to~\citet[Lemma~2.3, p.~27]{AK.HC}.
\begin{proposition}[Coarse-grained Poincar\'e inequality]
\label{p.coarse.grained.poincare}
For every~$s\in (0,1]$,~$q \in [1,\infty]$,~$m \in \Z$, every~$u\in H^1(\cu_m)$ and~$\h \in L^2_{\mathrm{sol}}(\cu_m)$,
\begin{equation}
	3^{-s m} [ \nabla u ]_{\Besov{-s}{2}{q}(\cu_m)}
	\leq
	(1-3^{-sq})^{-\nf1q}
	\lambda_{s,q}^{-\nf12}  (\cu_m; \a) \| \a^{\nf 12} \nabla u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_m)}
	\,,
	\label{e.besov.grad.poincare}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
	3^{-s m} [ \h ]_{\Besov{-s}{2}{q}(\cu_m)}
	\leq
	(1-3^{-sq})^{-\nf1q}
	\Lambda^{\nf12 }_{s,q} (\cu_m; \a)  \| \a^{-\nf 12} \h \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_m)}
	\,.
	\label{e.besov.flux.poincare}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

We combine the functional inequality
\begin{equation}
	[ u ]_{\underline{B}^{1-s}_{2,\infty}(\cu_0)}
	\leq
	C(d) [ \nabla u ]_{\Besov{-s}{2}{1}(\cu_0)}
	\,,
	\label{e.nabla.u.detach}
\end{equation}
with~\eqref{e.besov.grad.poincare} and the bound~$\| u - (u)_{\cu_0} \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_0)} \leq C \liminf_{s\uparrow 1}  [ u ]_{\underline{B}^{1-s}_{2,\infty}(\cu_0)}$, and let~$s\uparrow1$. This gives, for some~$C(d)<\infty$ and every~$u\in H^1(\cu_0)$,
\begin{equation}
	\| u - (u)_{\cu_0} \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_0)}
	\leq
	C
	\lambda_{1,1}^{-\nf12}  (\cu_0; \a) \| \a^{\nf 12} \nabla u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_0)}
	\,.
	\label{e.CG.Poincare.mean.zero}
\end{equation}
For zero boundary data we also have the following estimate. Let~$w\in H^1_0(\cu_0)$ be the \textbf{torsion function} of~$\cu_0$, that is, the solution of
\begin{equation*}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		& -\Delta w = 1 & \text{in} & \ \cu_0\,, \\
		& w = 0 & \text{on} & \ \partial \cu_0\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
\end{equation*}
Then, for every~$u\in H^1_0(\cu_0)$,
\begin{equation*}
	\bigl| ( u )_{\cu_0} \bigr|
	=
	\biggl| \fint_{\cu_0}
	\nabla w \cdot \nabla u
	\biggr|
	\leq
	C[ \nabla w ]_{\underline{B}^{1}_{2,\infty}(\cu_0)}
	[ \nabla u ]_{\Besov{-1}{2}{1}(\cu_0)}
	\leq
	C [ \nabla u ]_{\Besov{-1}{2}{1}(\cu_0)}\,.
\end{equation*}
Combining this with~\eqref{e.CG.Poincare.mean.zero} yields, for some~$C(d)<\infty$ and every~$u\in H^1_0(\cu_0)$,
\begin{equation}
	\| u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_0)}
	\leq
	C
	\lambda_{1,1}^{-\nf12}  (\cu_0; \a) \| \a^{\nf 12} \nabla u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_0)}
	\,.
	\label{e.CG.Poincare.trace.zero}
\end{equation}

\smallskip

% HC v3, highcontrast-arxiv-v3.tex, p.coarse.grained.Caccioppoli
% (Proposition 2.5), homogeneous case; adapted: forcing, t<1/2.
The homogeneous case of the next proposition is~\citet[Proposition~2.5, p.~29]{AK.HC}; the forcing term follows from Lemma~\ref{l.coarse.grained.Caccioppoli.RHS.ASD} below.
\begin{proposition}[Coarse-grained Caccioppoli inequality]
\label{p.coarse.grained.caccioppoli}
There exists a constant~$C(d)<\infty$ such that, for every~$s\in (0,1)$,~$t\in (0,\nf12)$ with~$s+t < 1$, every coefficient field~$\a \in L^{\infty}(\cu_0 ;\R_{\sym,+}^{d \times d})$ and every~$u \in H^1(\cu_0)$ and~$\f \in H^{2t}(\cu_0;\Rd)$ satisfying
\begin{equation*}
	-\nabla \cdot \a\nabla u = \nabla \cdot \f \quad \text{in} \ \cu_0
	\,,
\end{equation*}
we have the estimate
\begin{align*}
	\| \a^{\nf12} \nabla u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_{-1})}^2
	&
	\leq
	\biggl( \frac{C}{1-s-t} \biggr)^{\! 2 + \nf{4s}{(1-s-t)}}
	\biggl( \frac{\Lambda_{s,1}(\cu_0; \a)}{\lambda_{t,1}(\cu_0; \a)} \biggr)^{\!\nf{s}{(1-s-t)}}
	{\Lambda}_{s,1}(\cu_0; \a)
	\| u \|_{\underline{L}^2(\cu_{0})}^2
	\notag \\ & \qquad
	+
	t^{-11}
	\biggl( \frac{C}{1-s-t} \biggr)^{\! 2 + \nf{4s}{(1-s-t)}}
	\biggl( \frac{\Lambda_{s,1}(\cu_0; \a)}{\lambda_{t,1}(\cu_0; \a)} \biggr)^{\!\nf{(1-t)}{(1-s-t)}}
	\lambda_{t,1}^{-1} (\cu_0\,;\a)
	\| \f \|_{\underline{H}^{2t} (\cu_0)}^2
	\,.
\end{align*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Apply Lemma~\ref{l.coarse.grained.Caccioppoli.RHS.ASD} with zero boundary datum on each cube of a fixed finite cover of~$\cu_{-1}$ by cubes of side~$3^{-2}$ compactly contained in~$\cu_0$, and sum the resulting estimates. The forcing term has the displayed exponent since~$(1-t)/(1-s-t)=1+s/(1-s-t)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Sensitivity estimates}
\label{ss.sensitivity}

We quantify the dependence of~$J$,~$\a(U)$, and~$\a_*(U)$ on a scalar coefficient field.

\begin{lemma}
\label{l.sensitivity.general}
Let~$U$ be a bounded convex domain and let~$\a,\b:U\to(0,\infty)$ be scalar, uniformly elliptic coefficient fields. Then
\begin{equation*}
	\bigl|\b_*^{-\nf12}(U)\a_*(U)\b_*^{-\nf12}(U)-\Id\bigr|
	\leq\|\a\b^{-1}-1\|_{L^\infty(U)}\vee\|\a^{-1}\b-1\|_{L^\infty(U)}
	\,,
\end{equation*}
and
\begin{equation*}
	\bigl|\b^{-\nf12}(U)\a(U)\b^{-\nf12}(U)-\Id\bigr|
	\leq\|\a\b^{-1}-1\|_{L^\infty(U)}\vee\|\a^{-1}\b-1\|_{L^\infty(U)}
	\,.
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Both inequalities follow from the more precise bounds
\begin{equation}
	\left\{
	\begin{aligned}
		&
		\bigl( \inf_U (\a \b^{-1})  \bigr) \b(U) \leq\a(U) \leq \bigl( \sup_U (\a \b^{-1}) \bigr) \b(U)\,,
		\\
		&
		\bigl( \inf_U (\a^{-1} \b)  \bigr) \b^{-1}_*(U) \leq
		\a^{-1}_*(U) \leq
		\bigl( \sup_U (\a^{-1} \b )  \bigr)
		\b^{-1}_*(U)
		\,.
	\end{aligned}
	\right.
	\label{e.coarse-grained-matrix-inequalities.general}
\end{equation}
For the upper bound in the first line of~\eqref{e.coarse-grained-matrix-inequalities.general}, the variational formula~\eqref{e.variational.a} gives, for every~$e \in \Rd$,
\begin{align*}
	\frac{1}{2} e \cdot \a(U) e
	=
	\inf_{v \in e\cdot x + H_0^1(U)} \fint_{U} \frac{1}{2} \nabla v \cdot \a \nabla v
	&
	\leq \sup_U (\a \b^{-1})  \inf_{v \in e\cdot x + H_0^1(U)} \fint_{U} \frac{1}{2} \nabla v \cdot \b \nabla v
	\notag \\ &
	=
	\sup_U (\a \b^{-1})
	\frac{1}{2} e \cdot \b(U) e
	\,.
\end{align*}
The dual variational formula~\eqref{e.variational.astar} gives, for every~$e\in\Rd$,
\begin{align*}
	\frac{1}{2} e \cdot \a_*^{-1}(U) e
	=
	\min_{\h \in e + \Lsolo(U)} \fint_{U} \frac{1}{2} \h \cdot \a^{-1} \h
	&
	\leq
	\sup_U(  \a^{-1} \b )
	\min_{\h \in e + \Lsolo(U)} \fint_{U} \frac{1}{2} \h \cdot \b^{-1} \h
	\notag \\ &
	=
	\sup_U(  \a^{-1} \b )
	\frac{1}{2} e \cdot \b^{-1}_*(U) e
	\,.
\end{align*}
Interchanging~$\a$ and~$\b$ gives the lower bounds in~\eqref{e.coarse-grained-matrix-inequalities.general}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{l.J.sensitivity}
Let~$U\subseteq\Rd$ be a bounded convex domain and~$\a,\b:U\to(0,\infty)$ be scalar, uniformly elliptic coefficient fields. Then, for every~$\lambda \in (0,\infty)$,~$p,q \in \Rd$ and~$\delta \in (0,1]$,
\begin{align}
	\lefteqn{
	J(U, \lambda^{-\nf12}  p, \lambda ^{\nf12} q \, ; \b)
	}  \  &
	\notag \\ &
	\leq
	(1+\delta) J(U,p,q\,;\a)
	+
	\frac3{\delta} \Bigl(
	\| \lambda \b^{-1} \a - 1\|_{L^\infty(U)}^2  +
	\| \lambda^{-1} \b \a^{-1} - 1\|_{L^\infty(U)}^2
	\Bigr) \bigl( J(U,p,q\,; \a)  + p\cdot q \bigr)\,.
	\label{e.J.sensitivity}
\end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
